Yes, a validation runs at save time. It is in `erpnext/accounts/doctype/pricing_rule/pricing_rule.py`. **Call path:** `PricingRule.validate()` calls `validate_duplicate_apply_on()`. That method returns early if `apply_on` is "Transaction". Otherwise it takes the child table for the chosen `apply_on` (via `apply_on_dict`) and collects the non-empty values of the matching field. For "Item Code" that is the `item_code` field in the `items` table. I did not open `validate()` itself. The search results show it as the caller of `validate_duplicate_apply_on`. **Checks inside `validate_duplicate_apply_on`:** 1. **Exact duplicates.** If `len(values) != len(set(values))`, it throws "Duplicate {0} found in the table". This only catches the same code listed twice. It does not catch a template plus its variant. 2. **Template and variant conflict.** If `apply_on == "Item Code"`, it calls `validate_template_with_variant(values)`. **What `validate_template_with_variant(item_codes)` checks:** It runs one `frappe.get_all("Item", ...)` query with the filters `name in item_codes` and `variant_of in item_codes`. An Item matches when both of these hold: - Its own name is one of the rule's item codes. - Its `variant_of` field, which holds its template's name, is also one of the rule's item codes. Any match means a variant and its own template are both in the rule. The method then throws: "Variant {0} and its template {1} cannot both be added to the same Pricing Rule". The message uses the first matching row's `name` and `variant_of`, shown in bold. **Limits and test:** - The check only runs when Apply On is "Item Code". It does not run for Item Group or Brand. - It only looks at `variant_of`, so it catches a variant that is in the list together with the template it came from. - `test_pricing_rule_with_template_and_its_variant` in `test_pricing_rule.py` covers this case. It adds `_Test Variant Item` and `Test Variant PRT` and asserts that `rule.insert` raises `frappe.ValidationError`. **Why the rule exists:** At pricing time, `_get_pricing_rules` in `utils.py` matches a rule on the item's own code or on its `variant_of` template code. A rule listing both would be ambiguous, so it is rejected when saved.

final answer
ERPNext breaks same-timestamp ties with the `creation` timestamp of each Stock Ledger Entry (SLE). Every ordering and "previous/future" query uses `(posting_datetime, creation)` as a composite key. All of this is in `erpnext/stock/stock_ledger.py`. I read the code but did not run any scenario. **Ordering key** - `posting_datetime` is the combined posting date and time. - `creation` is the row's insert timestamp, which acts as the tie-breaker. - `sort_sles()` in the repost class sorts the queued SLEs by `(get_datetime(posting_datetime), get_datetime(creation))`. Reposting walks entries in that order from a `deque` in `_sles`. **Selecting the "previous" SLE** - `get_previous_sle_of_current_voucher(args, operator="<", exclude_current_voucher=False)` is the main function. - It queries non-cancelled SLEs (`is_cancelled = 0`) for the same item and warehouse. - It orders by `posting_datetime desc, creation desc`, takes `limit 1` and uses `for update`. - It has three branches for the voucher being processed: - **Normal repost of an existing SLE.** When `creation` and `sle_id` are given and the entry is not `cancelled`, it switches the operator to `<=` and adds `creation < '<this creation>'`. At an equal timestamp, only entries created before the current one count as "previous". The current entry and any later-created entry are excluded. - **Cancellation.** When `cancelled` is set, that branch is skipped. The query is then a plain `posting_datetime < …`, so entries at the same timestamp are not used as the baseline. Everything at that timestamp gets re-evaluated instead. - **Stock Reconciliation.** `exclude_current_voucher=True` adds `voucher_no != current` for reconciliation shifts. - `get_previous_sle()` and `get_stock_ledger_entries()` use the same `posting_datetime {op}, creation {order}` ordering. They also exclude the SLE itself with `name != …` when the operator is `>` or `<=`. **Selecting the "current" and same-timestamp entries** - `get_sle_against_current_voucher(kwargs)` fetches the SLEs at exactly `posting_datetime ==`, ordered by `creation asc` with `for_update`. - If the voucher is not cancelled, it also requires `creation == kwargs.creation`, so only that exact entry is returned. - If the voucher is being cancelled, there is no `creation` filter. It returns every non-cancelled SLE at that timestamp, in creation order, so none of the survivors are skipped when reposting. **Updating future quantities** - `update_qty_in_future_sle()` builds its `future_condition` as `posting_datetime > X`. For a non-cancelled entry it adds `OR (posting_datetime == X AND creation > this.creation)`. - On cancellation (`is_cancelled` set), the same-timestamp clause is dropped. Cancelled rows already have `is_cancelled = 1`, so they are filtered out. - `get_next_stock_reco()` and `get_next_reco_datetime_condition()` use the same rule to find the next Stock Reconciliation boundary: - For a cancel, the condition is `posting_datetime >= current`. - Otherwise it is `> current` or `== current AND creation > this.creation`. - `get_datetime_limit_condition()` bounds the update range with `< datetime` or `== datetime AND creation < reco.creation`. **Negative-stock validation** - `update_qty_in_future_sle()` ends by calling `validate_negative_qty_in_future_sle()`. - That calls `get_future_sle_with_negative_qty()`, which looks for non-cancelled SLEs of the same item and warehouse with `posting_datetime >= X`, `voucher_no != current` and `qty_after_transaction < 0`. It orders by `posting_datetime, creation` and takes the first. - Because `qty_after_transaction` was just recomputed in creation order, a same-second Purchase Receipt and Material Transfer cannot appear in the wrong order. The error is raised only if the first negative balance is below zero after rounding to float precision (`is_negative_with_precision`). - Batch quantities are checked in `get_future_sle_with_negative_batch_qty()`. It uses a window function, `sum(actual_qty) over (order by posting_datetime, creation)`.

final answer
ERPNext breaks same-timestamp ties with the Stock Ledger Entry's `creation` timestamp, not with `posting_date` and `posting_time` alone. The code is in `erpnext/stock/stock_ledger.py`. I read the functions below but did not trace `validate_negative_qty_in_future_sle`, so what it does with the result is inferred. **1. Combined timestamp.** `get_combine_datetime(posting_date, posting_time)` in `erpnext/stock/utils.py` builds one `posting_datetime`. All comparisons use that column. Two entries posted in the same second have equal `posting_datetime`, so a second sort key is needed. **2. Tie-break by `creation`.** Every query that has to separate same-`posting_datetime` rows adds `creation` as the second key: - **`get_previous_sle_of_current_voucher(args, operator="<", exclude_current_voucher=False)`** - This finds the "previous" entry. It sorts `posting_datetime desc, creation desc` and takes `limit 1 ... for update`, so it returns the nearest earlier entry for that item and warehouse. - It always filters `is_cancelled = 0`, so cancelled rows are never picked as the previous entry. - If `args` carries `creation` and `sle_id` and the voucher is not being cancelled, it switches to `<=` on the timestamp and adds `creation < <this creation>`. Same-second rows created earlier count as "previous", and later ones do not. - With `exclude_current_voucher=True` it adds `voucher_no != <current voucher>`. This is a different mechanism that excludes the voucher by name instead of by `creation`. - **`get_sle_against_current_voucher(kwargs)`** - This finds the "current" entries. It selects non-cancelled rows with the same item, warehouse and `posting_datetime`, ordered by `creation asc`, `for_update`. - When the voucher is not cancelled, it also requires `creation == kwargs.creation`, which pins the exact entry. - When `cancelled` is set, that filter is dropped. All same-timestamp live entries are then re-processed in creation order. The cancelled voucher's rows are already `is_cancelled = 1`, so they are excluded. - `update_entries_after.process_sle_against_current_timestamp` calls this and runs `process_sle` on each row, with `sle["timestamp"] = sle.posting_datetime`. - **`update_qty_in_future_sle`** - The "future" set is `posting_datetime > X`. For a non-cancelled entry that has `creation`, it also includes `(posting_datetime == X) & (creation > this creation)`. - Same-second entries created later therefore get their `qty_after_transaction` shifted, and earlier ones do not. - For a cancellation (`is_cancelled` set), the second condition is skipped. Every non-cancelled entry at that same timestamp is treated as downstream of the cancelled row and recalculated. - It ends by calling `validate_negative_qty_in_future_sle`. That check therefore sees balances in a deterministic order, not one that depends on how the database happens to return tied rows. - **Batch and serial valuation** (`erpnext/stock/deprecated_serial_batch.py`) - `get_last_sle_for_non_batch` and `get_sle_for_batches` use `posting_datetime < X OR (posting_datetime == X AND creation < sle.creation)`. - If the SLE has no `creation` yet, they add 1 ms to the timestamp so the new row sorts after everything already at that second. **Effect on your example.** Suppose a Purchase Receipt and a Material Transfer share a second, and one is later cancelled. The cancelled voucher's rows are marked `is_cancelled = 1` and drop out of every query. The remaining same-second entries are replayed in `creation` order, and each one's "previous" entry is the latest non-cancelled row earlier by `(posting_datetime, creation)`. The running quantity used for negative-stock validation is then computed from a fixed sequence. The ordering is only as good as `creation`. Rows with an identical `creation` would still tie.

final answer
Ties on the same timestamp are broken by each Stock Ledger Entry's `creation` timestamp. This logic is in `erpnext/stock/stock_ledger.py`. I read the code but didn't run any scenario. **Ordering key** - Every SLE has a combined `posting_datetime`, built from the posting date and time by `get_combine_datetime`. When two entries share it, `creation` is the tie-breaker. - `get_previous_sle_of_current_voucher` (about line 1929) orders by `posting_datetime desc, creation desc` with `limit 1`. - It only looks at `is_cancelled = 0` rows. - It uses `for update`, which locks the row it returns. - `get_stock_ledger_entries` (about line 2071) uses the same pair of columns, `order by posting_datetime {order}, creation {order}`. `get_previous_sle` calls it with `<=` and `desc`. **Selecting "previous" for the current entry** - In `get_previous_sle_of_current_voucher`, the default operator is `<`. - If the arguments carry `creation` and `sle_id` and the entry is not cancelled, the function switches to `posting_datetime <= X` and adds `and creation < '<creation>'`. This returns the entry created just before the current one at the same timestamp. - That `creation` filter is skipped on cancellation, because `args["cancelled"]` is set. The cancelled voucher's own SLEs are already marked `is_cancelled = 1`, so they drop out of the query anyway. - For a Stock Reconciliation, `exclude_current_voucher=True` adds `voucher_no != current`, so the reconciliation's own entries are never picked as "previous". **Selecting "future" entries to update** - `update_qty_in_future_sle` (about line 2212) selects entries where `posting_datetime > X`. - If `creation` is present and the entry is not cancelled, it adds `or (posting_datetime == X and creation > creation)`. - So when a Purchase Receipt and a Material Transfer share a timestamp, the one created later counts as "after" the earlier one. - On cancellation (`is_cancelled` set), the same-timestamp clause is dropped. Only strictly later `posting_datetime` rows are shifted this way. **Same-timestamp lookups used during reposting** - A helper around lines 2305–2351 uses `(posting_datetime == current) & (creation > kwargs.creation)` to find later same-time entries. Another uses `(posting_datetime == X) & (creation < detail.creation)` to find earlier ones. - `get_sle_by_voucher_detail_no` and the helper near line 2584 take an optional `creation` and add `posting_datetime == X and creation < creation`. - Window sums, such as the cumulative total over `order by posting_datetime, creation` (about line 2450), use the same ordering. **Reposting flow** - `update_entries_after.__init__` and `process_sle` (about lines 679–997) seed the running balance from `get_previous_sle_of_current_voucher(sle)`, cached per (item, warehouse) in `prev_sle_dict`. - Reposting then replays SLEs in `posting_datetime, creation` order and checks the negative-stock rule on each. That is how the Purchase Receipt versus Material Transfer order stays deterministic after one of them is cancelled. - When the repost entry reaches the same-timestamp entries, the cancelled voucher's rows are skipped and the survivors keep their original `creation` order. **Where the check sits** - `StockLedgerEntry.validate` and `get_available_qty_after_prev_transaction` in `stock_ledger_entry.py` handle the per-entry validation. - The `creation` tie-break keeps the balance before each entry unambiguous. If a Material Transfer was created after its Purchase Receipt, it always sees the receipt's stock, and cancelling the receipt makes the transfer fail the negative-stock check.

final answer
ERPNext breaks same-timestamp ties with the `creation` timestamp, not with the posting date and time alone. It does this in a few places. The sections below take them in turn, and the tie-break is the same in all of them. Everything is in `erpnext/stock/stock_ledger.py`. **1. Ordering key** Stock Ledger Entries are ordered by the pair `(posting_datetime, creation)`. `posting_datetime` is `posting_date` and `posting_time` combined by `get_combine_datetime`. `creation` is the row's insert timestamp, so it separates entries that share a posting second. - `get_stock_ledger_entries(previous_sle, operator, order, limit, for_update, ...)` filters on `is_cancelled = 0` and `posting_datetime <op> %(posting_datetime)s`. It sorts with `order by posting_datetime {order}, creation {order}`. - `update_entries_after.sort_sles` sorts the reposting queue with `key=(get_datetime(d.posting_datetime), get_datetime(d.creation))`. - `get_balance_qty_from_sle` in `stock_balance.py` uses `order_by="posting_datetime desc, creation desc"`. **2. "Previous" entry** `get_previous_sle` calls `get_stock_ledger_entries` with `"<="`, `"desc"` and `limit 1`. It gets the last entry on or before the current time bucket. If a reference `sle` name is passed in, `args["name"]` is set, and the `name != %(name)s` condition is added so the entry doesn't match itself. `get_previous_sle_of_current_voucher` is the one reposting uses to seed `prev_sle_dict`. Its default operator is `<`, which is strictly before the timestamp. - When the call has `creation` and `sle_id` and is not a cancellation, it switches to `<=` with `creation < '<creation>'`. - That means "same second, but created earlier than me". - With `exclude_current_voucher`, it adds `voucher_no != ...`. - Cancelled entries are always excluded with `is_cancelled = 0`. **3. "Current" entries** `get_sle_against_current_voucher` selects entries with `posting_datetime == kwargs.posting_datetime`, ordered by `creation asc`, with `for update`. - On a normal post, it also requires `creation == kwargs.creation`. That isolates exactly the entry being posted and not its same-second siblings. - On cancellation (`kwargs.cancelled` set), the `creation` filter is dropped. All live entries in that same second are then re-processed in `creation` order. Previously they might have been valued against the entry that has now been cancelled. `update_entries_after.build` then runs one of two paths: - **Single entry (`sle_id` present):** it calls `process_sle_against_current_timestamp`. - **Reposting (no `sle_id`):** it calls `repost_stock_ledgers`. That fetches future entries with `get_future_entries_to_repost`, which uses `>=` and `asc`, and then `sort_sles`. It tracks `distinct_sles` by name so an entry isn't processed twice. **4. Negative-stock validation** Both validators skip cancelled entries and use the same two-part ordering. - `update_entries_after.validate_negative_stock` runs per entry during replay. It computes `wh_data.qty_after_transaction + actual_qty - reserved_stock`, rounded to system precision. It records an exception when the result is below a small threshold. Because entries replay in `(posting_datetime, creation)` order, a Purchase Receipt and a Material Transfer at the same second are evaluated in the order they were created. Once the cancelled voucher's entries are `is_cancelled = 1`, they are no longer in the running balance. - `validate_negative_qty_in_future_sle` calls `get_future_sle_with_negative_qty`. That looks for the first non-cancelled entry with `qty_after_transaction < 0` where `posting_datetime >= ours` and `voucher_no != ours`, ordered by `posting_datetime` then `creation`. `get_future_sle_with_negative_batch_qty` does the same for batches. It uses a window sum `over (order by posting_datetime, creation)`, so the running total is deterministic within a second. **5. Reposting entry point** `repost_future_sle` builds `update_entries_after({... "posting_date", "posting_time", "creation", ...})` for each item and warehouse. The `creation` of the triggering entry travels with the arguments. That lets the previous and current queries above place the reposting start point exactly among same-second entries.

final answer
For an internal transfer, the Purchase Receipt takes the rate that was stored on the Delivery Note Item when the Delivery Note was made. It does not recompute the rate at receipt time. The one exception is a receipt that has no link to a Delivery Note Item. I read the code but did not run it. **Where the rate comes from** All of this is in `erpnext/controllers/buying_controller.py`, in `BuyingController`. 1. `set_sales_incoming_rate_for_internal_transfer()` fills `sales_incoming_rate` on each item row. It is called from the internal-transfer validation, which first checks `is_internal_transfer()`. 2. If the row has a `delivery_note_item` link, it reads the stored value from the database with `frappe.db.get_value("Delivery Note Item", <delivery_note_item>, field)`. - It reads `incoming_rate` when the receipt is for an internal supplier (`is_internal_supplier`). That is the source warehouse's valuation rate, saved on the Delivery Note Item when the Delivery Note was submitted. - Otherwise it reads `rate`. - The value is multiplied by the row's `conversion_factor`. 3. If the row has no `delivery_note_item` link, the code falls back to a fresh calculation. It calls `get_incoming_rate()` from `erpnext/stock/utils.py` for the `from_warehouse` at the receipt's posting date and time, with a negative quantity, and uses the result as `sales_incoming_rate`. This is the only case where the rate is computed at receipt time. **How the rate is applied** - Unless the Stock Settings option `allow_internal_transfer_at_arms_length_price` is on, the validation resets the discount and margin fields to 0 and overwrites `d.rate` with `sales_incoming_rate`. It shows the message "Item rate has been updated as per valuation rate since its an internal stock transfer." - `update_valuation_rate()` uses `sales_incoming_rate` in place of the net amount: `net_rate = item.qty * item.sales_incoming_rate`. It then adds the item's tax and landed-cost amounts and divides by the quantity in stock UOM to get `item.valuation_rate`. - When the stock ledger entries are built, the inward entry gets `incoming_rate = d.valuation_rate`. It is flagged `recalculate_rate = 1` when `from_warehouse` is set. - The `recalculate_rate` flag together with `dependant_sle_voucher_detail_no` lets the stock ledger re-value the entry if the source side is later reposted. So the rate is anchored to the Delivery Note's stored value, but it can still be adjusted by a stock reposting.

final answer
The system takes the incoming rate from a value fixed on the Delivery Note. It does not recompute the rate at receipt time from the source warehouse. The one exception is a Purchase Receipt item that isn't linked to a Delivery Note item. **1. Item rate on the Purchase Receipt** - `BuyingController.set_incoming_rate` in `erpnext/controllers/buying_controller.py` handles this. It only runs for a non-return Purchase Receipt or Purchase Invoice that is an internal transfer. - It calls `set_sales_incoming_rate_for_internal_transfer`. - If the item has a `delivery_note_item` link, which is the normal case when the receipt is made with `make_inter_company_purchase_receipt`, it reads the Delivery Note Item's stored `incoming_rate` straight from the database. It multiplies that by the conversion factor. For a Purchase Invoice it reads the Sales Invoice Item instead. - The code reads `incoming_rate` when the supplier is an internal supplier and `rate` otherwise. The result goes into `sales_incoming_rate`. - If the item has no Delivery Note Item link, it computes a fresh rate. It calls `get_incoming_rate` for the item's `from_warehouse` at the receipt's posting date and time, with a negative quantity. - Unless Stock Settings has `allow_internal_transfer_at_arms_length_price` enabled, `set_incoming_rate` then: - sets the item's `rate` to `sales_incoming_rate`; - zeroes discount and margin; - shows the message "Item rate has been updated as per valuation rate…". **2. Stock ledger entry (the valuation rate actually booked)** - `update_entries_after.get_incoming_outgoing_rate_from_transaction` in `erpnext/stock/stock_ledger.py` handles this. For a Purchase Receipt or Purchase Invoice that is an internal transfer, it calls `get_incoming_rate_for_inter_company_transfer(sle)`. - That function finds the linked Delivery Note Item and returns its stored `incoming_rate`. - It then adds the landed cost voucher amount per unit: `landed_cost_voucher_amount / abs(actual_qty)`. **Conclusion** The rate is anchored to the `incoming_rate` saved on the Delivery Note Item when the Delivery Note was submitted. That rate is the outgoing valuation of the source warehouse at the time. The receipt keeps using it even if the Purchase Receipt item's own `valuation_rate` is wrong or later changed. The test `test_internal_transfer_pr_incoming_sle_anchored_to_dn_rate` in `test_purchase_receipt.py` checks this. It forces the Purchase Receipt item's `valuation_rate` to 80, and a repost still restores the Delivery Note's rate of 100. The only part that changes after the Delivery Note is landed cost added on the Purchase Receipt side.

final answer
The Purchase Receipt uses the incoming rate that was stored on the source Delivery Note Item. It does not compute a new rate from the receiving warehouse. **Where it happens:** `erpnext/stock/stock_ledger.py`, in the stock ledger entry (SLE) processing for a Purchase Receipt or Purchase Invoice. 1. **Detecting an internal transfer.** In `update_entries_after.process_sle` (around line 1032), the system checks that the voucher is a Purchase Receipt or Purchase Invoice with a `voucher_detail_no`, and that `is_internal_transfer(sle)` is true. `is_internal_transfer` requires `is_internal_supplier` to be set and `represents_company` to equal `company` (around line 2571). 2. **Overriding the rate.** For such a voucher, the code calls `get_incoming_rate_for_inter_company_transfer(sle)`. If `actual_qty < 0`, it sets `sle.outgoing_rate` to the result. Otherwise, if the rate is non-zero, it sets `sle.incoming_rate`. The in-code comment explains why. It anchors the receipt to the DN/SI incoming rate so that a stale Purchase Receipt `valuation_rate` doesn't leak the difference into COGS (cost of goods sold) through `divisional_loss`. 3. **How the rate is looked up.** `get_incoming_rate_for_inter_company_transfer` (around line 2540) works in three steps: - It reads the `delivery_note_item` field from the Purchase Receipt Item. For a Purchase Invoice flow it reads `sales_invoice_item`. - It then fetches `incoming_rate` from that Delivery Note Item, or from the Sales Invoice Item, using `frappe.get_cached_value`. - It adds `landed_cost_voucher_amount / abs(actual_qty)` from the receipt item, so landed costs are included. 4. **The same rule in the other path.** In `get_incoming_outgoing_rate_from_transaction` (around line 1467), the same function is used for internal-transfer Purchase Receipts and Purchase Invoices. Ordinary receipts use the item's `valuation_rate`. **Fixed or computed fresh:** the base rate is the Delivery Note Item's `incoming_rate`, so it is tied to the source document. That value was computed and saved when the Delivery Note's outgoing stock ledger entry was valued. For serial/batch items, around line 1461, the code writes the rate back to the Delivery Note Item with `frappe.db.set_value(..., "incoming_rate", rate)`. The Purchase Receipt only reads that stored field and does not revalue. The final rate is not strictly frozen, because the landed cost voucher amount on the receipt item is added each time the rate is looked up. The Delivery Note Item's `incoming_rate` can also be updated if the source Delivery Note's stock entries are reposted.

final answer
For an internal transfer, the Purchase Receipt uses the rate fixed on the source Delivery Note item. It is not recomputed from the receiving warehouse. One exception applies: a PR item with no Delivery Note link gets a rate computed fresh at receipt time. I read the code but did not run it or the test I mention below. **1. Rate on the Purchase Receipt document** This happens in `BuyingController.set_sales_incoming_rate_for_internal_transfer` (`erpnext/controllers/buying_controller.py`, ~line 647). It is called from the internal-transfer rate validation. - **Linked to a Delivery Note:** If the PR item has a `delivery_note_item` link, the code reads `incoming_rate` from that Delivery Note Item. It multiplies by the item's `conversion_factor` and stores the result in `d.sales_incoming_rate`. For a Purchase Invoice, the source is the Sales Invoice Item instead. The field is `incoming_rate` when `is_internal_supplier` is set. Otherwise it is `rate`. - **No Delivery Note link:** The code calls `get_incoming_rate(...)` from `erpnext/stock/utils.py`. That uses the `from_warehouse`, posting date and time, and qty to compute the rate at that moment. This is the one fresh computation. - **Item rate:** Unless "Allow Internal Transfer at Arm's Length Price" is enabled in Stock Settings, the item `rate` is overwritten with `sales_incoming_rate`. Discounts and margins are zeroed. The user sees a message that the rate was updated as per valuation rate. **2. Rate on the Stock Ledger Entry** This is the rate that actually determines the stock valuation. In `erpnext/stock/stock_ledger.py`, the SLE rate logic (~lines 1032–1044 and ~1467–1472) checks whether the voucher is a Purchase Receipt or Purchase Invoice and whether `is_internal_transfer(sle)` is true. If so, it calls `get_incoming_rate_for_inter_company_transfer(sle)` (~line 2540). That function: - finds the PR item's `delivery_note_item` (or `sales_invoice_item`); - reads the stored `incoming_rate` from that Delivery Note Item (or Sales Invoice Item); - adds the landed cost voucher amount per unit, `landed_cost_voucher_amount / abs(actual_qty)`, if the PR item has one; - returns `rate + lcv_rate`. For a negative SLE (the outgoing leg) the result is set as `outgoing_rate`. For an inward SLE it is set as `incoming_rate`. A code comment explains the reason: without this anchor, a stale PR `valuation_rate` would leak the difference into COGS. `test_internal_transfer_pr_incoming_sle_anchored_to_dn_rate` in `test_purchase_receipt.py` is named after this behaviour. The Delivery Note's `incoming_rate` was calculated when that Delivery Note was submitted, using the source warehouse's valuation at that time. The Purchase Receipt just reads that stored value. The only adjustment at receipt time is the landed cost add-on.
Q19Group D — Numeric/logic edge casesWhen a Purchase Invoice is created against a Standard Cost item's Purchase Receipt with "Update Stock" switched off, what GL entri…
When a Purchase Invoice is created against a Standard Cost item's Purchase Receipt with "Update Stock" switched off, what GL entries does the invoice post for the item's expense side, and how is that different from an invoice with Update Stock enabled?

final answer
**Update Stock off.** The invoice posts one expense-side GL entry per item. The code is in `PurchaseInvoice` GL composition, in `make_item_gl_entries` in `erpnext/accounts/doctype/purchase_invoice/services/gl_composer.py`. - Because `update_stock` is false, the item takes the `else` branch (around lines 319–350). That branch debits `item.expense_account` for the full billed amount (`base_amount` from `tax_service.get_amount_and_base_amount`). The credit side is the supplier payable. - For stock items linked to a Purchase Receipt, `item.expense_account` is normally Stock Received But Not Billed (SRBNB). The invoice therefore just clears SRBNB at the full billed amount. - The code comment in that branch explains why. The Purchase Receipt already booked the stock at standard cost and the Purchase Price Variance (PPV). Booking PPV again would double count it and leave SRBNB partly uncleared. - If the company has provisional accounting for non-stock items, `make_provisional_gl_entry` adds its own entries. - If the exchange rate differs from the receipt's, an extra entry debits the expense account and posts the difference to the exchange gain/loss account. **Update Stock on.** The item takes the first branch, which applies when `update_stock` and `auto_accounting_for_stock` are both on and the item is a stock item or fixed asset. The invoice posts the stock itself, so it creates Stock Ledger Entries. `make_stock_adjustment_entry` then does the following: - It computes `warehouse_debit_amount` as `valuation_rate × qty × conversion_factor`. This is the standard-cost value that goes into the stock account. - It compares that amount with the actual stock value difference from the Stock Ledger Entries (`voucher_wise_stock_value`). - If the two differ, it posts a separate "Stock Adjustment" debit. The account comes from `get_stock_variance_account`. For a Standard Cost item (`get_valuation_method == "Standard Cost"`), that is the PPV account from `get_purchase_price_variance_account` in `item_standard_cost.py`. For other items it is the company's `default_expense_account`. - The debit amount is `warehouse_debit_amount − stock_amount`. For returns without a `return_against`, it is `stock_amount − warehouse_debit_amount`. - `item.expense_account` is then debited with the adjusted `warehouse_debit_amount`, not the billed amount. The code does this for the normal, non-`from_warehouse` case and skips it for internal transfers. - Landed cost entries, subcontracting raw-material supplier-warehouse credits, and `from_warehouse` transfer entries are posted too. **Difference.** With Update Stock off, the invoice has no PPV or stock adjustment line. It only debits the expense/SRBNB account for the billed amount against the supplier, and the receipt keeps the PPV. With Update Stock on, the invoice values the stock at standard cost. It posts the expense debit at that value, plus a separate PPV debit for any gap between the standard value and the actual stock value.

final answer
I've read the Purchase Invoice GL composer and I'm ready to answer. **Update Stock OFF (the case you asked about)** The code is in `PurchaseInvoiceGLComposer.make_item_gl_entries` in `erpnext/accounts/doctype/purchase_invoice/services/gl_composer.py`. The `update_stock and auto_accounting_for_stock` branch is skipped, so execution falls to the `else` branch (lines ~319–350). For the item's expense side the invoice posts one entry: - **Debit** `item.expense_account`, which for a stock item is normally Stock Received But Not Billed. The amount is the full billed `base_amount`. The `against` is the supplier. The supplier payable credit is posted separately by `make_supplier_gl_entry`. It posts no Purchase Price Variance (PPV) entry and no inventory entry. The code comment at lines 332–336 explains why. The linked Purchase Receipt already booked the stock at the standard cost and booked the PPV. The invoice only clears SRBNB at the full billed amount against the supplier. Booking PPV again would double count it and leave SRBNB partly uncleared. Two smaller entries can still appear: - If the exchange rate differs from the one on the Purchase Receipt, there is an extra debit to the expense account and a credit to the exchange gain/loss account. - If valuation taxes were not booked in the Purchase Receipt, there is a debit to SRBNB for `item_tax_amount`. **Update Stock ON** The `update_stock and auto_accounting_for_stock` branch runs for stock items, and `make_stock_adjustment_entry` is called first. - It computes `warehouse_debit_amount = valuation_rate × qty × conversion_factor`. This is the stock-valued amount, not the billed amount. - If that differs from the Stock Ledger Entry's `stock_value_difference`, it posts an adjustment debit. For a Standard Cost item, `get_stock_variance_account` sends this to the Purchase Price Variance account, which comes from `get_purchase_price_variance_account` in `item_standard_cost.py`. For other items it goes to the company's default expense account. - It then debits `item.expense_account` for `warehouse_debit_amount`. For a transfer from another warehouse it debits the full `base_net_amount` instead. - The stock-in-hand entries themselves come from the stock ledger posting, not from this method. - Landed-cost credits and subcontracting raw-material-supplier credits are also posted in this branch. For returns with Update Stock on, the same variance logic compares the returned stock amount with the warehouse value, and the PPV account is used for Standard Cost items. **Difference** - **Update Stock off:** the invoice only does the billing side. It debits SRBNB (or whatever the expense account is) for the full billed amount and has no PPV line. - **Update Stock on:** the invoice also receives the stock itself. The expense side is split into the stock-valued amount plus a separate Purchase Price Variance debit for Standard Cost items. I did not run this against a live Standard Cost item. This comes from reading the code.

final answer
**Update Stock off (invoice against a Purchase Receipt):** the invoice posts a single expense-side entry per item, with no PPV line. The code is in `PurchaseInvoice`'s GL composer, `erpnext/accounts/doctype/purchase_invoice/services/gl_composer.py`. It is the `else` branch of the item loop, used when `doc.update_stock` is false. For each item it does the following: - **Debit:** `item.expense_account` for the full billed amount (`base_amount`, from `tax_service.get_amount_and_base_amount`). `against` is the supplier. Because the invoice is linked to a Purchase Receipt, that account is Stock Received But Not Billed. This clears the liability the receipt created. - **Credit:** the supplier (Creditors) payable is posted separately, in the party-side entries. - **No Purchase Price Variance entry.** The code comment says the Purchase Receipt already booked the stock at standard cost and the Purchase Price Variance. Booking the variance again would double count it and leave Stock Received But Not Billed partly uncleared. - **Exchange-rate difference:** if the invoice's exchange rate differs from the receipt's, there is an extra pair of entries, a debit to the expense account and a credit to the company's `exchange_gain_loss_account`. This applies only when `adjust_incoming_rate` is off and the rate matches the receipt's rate. - **Provisional accounting:** if enabled for non-stock items, `make_provisional_gl_entry` reverses the receipt's provisional entry. **Update Stock on:** the invoice acts as the receipt itself. The item goes through `make_stock_adjustment_entry`, which returns `warehouse_debit_amount`. - **Debit:** `item.expense_account` for `warehouse_debit_amount`, which is `valuation_rate × qty × conversion_factor`. For a Standard Cost item this is the standard-cost value, not the billed amount. If `from_warehouse` is set, the inventory entries are posted instead, as shown in the code. - **Variance debit:** if the stock value from the Stock Ledger (`voucher_wise_stock_value`) differs from the billed or valuation amount, a "Stock Adjustment" debit is posted. It goes to `get_stock_variance_account(item)`. For items whose valuation method is "Standard Cost", that is the Purchase Price Variance account from `get_purchase_price_variance_account`. For other items it is the company's `default_expense_account`. - The Stock-in-Hand debit comes from the Stock Ledger entries that the invoice creates. The invoice also credits landed-cost accounts and the supplier warehouse (for `rm_supp_cost`) when those apply. The difference is that with Update Stock off, the invoice only reverses Stock Received But Not Billed at the billed amount and leaves the Purchase Price Variance on the Purchase Receipt. With Update Stock on, the invoice itself books stock at standard cost and posts the Purchase Price Variance.

    {"pattern": "def make_stock_adjustment_entry", "path": "/benchmarks/dataset/repos/erpnext-colby/erpnext/accounts/doctype/purchase_invoice/services/gl_composer.py", "output_mode": "content", "-A": 60}

final answer
With Update Stock off, the invoice posts only one expense-side GL entry per item: a debit to the item's expense account for the full billed amount. With Update Stock on, it posts a stock-side debit and can add a Purchase Price Variance (PPV) entry. All of this is in `PurchaseInvoiceGLComposer.make_item_gl_entries` in `erpnext/accounts/doctype/purchase_invoice/services/gl_composer.py`. I didn't run it. I also didn't read the rest of `get_stock_variance_account`, which picks the variance account. **Update Stock off** - The invoice goes through the `else` branch, which treats it as having no stock impact. A comment in the code says the linked Purchase Receipt already booked the stock at standard cost and the PPV. - The branch debits `item.expense_account` for the full billed amount (`base_amount` from `tax_service.get_amount_and_base_amount`), with `against` set to the supplier. The expense account is the item's `deferred_expense_account` instead if deferred expense is enabled and the invoice isn't a return. - With perpetual inventory, that expense account is normally Stock Received But Not Billed (SRBNB). So this debit clears SRBNB at the billed amount, and the credit to the supplier payable is posted separately. - The code deliberately does not book PPV here, because that would double count it and leave SRBNB partly uncleared. - Two extras can appear: - If provisional accounting for non-stock items is on, `make_provisional_gl_entry` reverses the provisional entry. - If the exchange rate has changed since the receipt, there is an exchange gain/loss pair: a debit to the expense account and a credit to the company's `exchange_gain_loss_account`. This happens only when `set_landed_cost_based_on_purchase_invoice_rate` is off and the net rate matches the receipt's. **Update Stock on (with perpetual inventory and a stock item)** - This uses the first branch, `update_stock and auto_accounting_for_stock`. `make_stock_adjustment_entry` computes `warehouse_debit_amount = valuation_rate × qty × conversion_factor`. The invoice writes its own Stock Ledger Entries, and the debit goes to the item's `expense_account` at that valuation amount, not at the billed `base_net_amount`. In this branch `expense_account` is the item's stock or warehouse inventory account, but the code does not show that setup, so this is inferred. - If that valuation amount differs from the stock value in the Stock Ledger Entry, `make_stock_adjustment_entry` adds a separate debit for the difference. It goes to the account from `get_stock_variance_account`, which the docstring says is the PPV account for Standard Cost items and the default expense account for others. The difference is the billed valuation amount minus the Stock Ledger Entry value. - On a return that is not linked to an original invoice, it books the equivalent adjustment in the other direction. - If an item has a `from_warehouse`, it also posts a debit to the target inventory account and a negative debit to the source warehouse's inventory account. Landed cost voucher amounts and subcontracting raw-material supplier-warehouse costs are credited as well. In short, Update Stock off books only the SRBNB-clearing debit at the billed amount, and the PPV stays on the Purchase Receipt. Update Stock on books the stock-side debit at valuation, plus a separate PPV debit when billed and valuation amounts differ.
Q20Group D — Numeric/logic edge casesSeveral ERPNext queries pick a single "latest" row using ORDER BY some-date DESC with LIMIT 1. How does the codebase guarantee tho…
Several ERPNext queries pick a single "latest" row using ORDER BY some-date DESC with LIMIT 1. How does the codebase guarantee those picks are deterministic when several rows share the same date — what is the pattern, why does it matter across database engines, and where is it applied? Name concrete functions and files.

final answer
**The pattern.** The date is never the only sort key. A second, unique or near-unique column is added as a tiebreaker, and it is sorted in the same direction as the date. The tiebreaker is usually `creation`, or `name` in the currency case. Examples are `order_by="date desc, name desc"` and `order_by="effective_date desc, creation desc"`. The query ends with `limit=1`, and the code then reads `[0]`. In the query-builder code the same idea is chained: `.orderby(date, order=Order.desc).orderby(name, ...)`. **Why it matters across engines.** A sort on a date alone is not a total order when rows share a date. SQL does not define which of the tied rows comes first, and `LIMIT 1` just takes whichever one the engine's plan yields. MariaDB/MySQL (InnoDB) and PostgreSQL can choose different plans, index scans or sort algorithms, so they can return different rows for the same data. The same engine can also change its answer after a plan change or an index change. Adding `creation` or `name` makes the order total, so every engine returns the same row. I didn't open any code that states this reasoning. It is my inference from the pattern, not something the repository documents. **Where it is applied** (found by grep, not exhaustively verified): - `erpnext/setup/utils.py`, `get_exchange_rate`: the latest Currency Exchange row, using `order_by="date desc, name desc"` with `limit=1`. - `erpnext/stock/doctype/item_standard_cost/item_standard_cost.py`: - `ItemStandardCost.get_last_standard_cost` uses `"effective_date desc, creation desc"` with `limit=1`. - `get_item_standard_rate` uses the same ordering to find the rate effective on a posting date. - `erpnext/stock/stock_balance.py`: - `get_balance_qty_from_sle` uses `"posting_datetime desc, creation desc"` with `limit=1` to take the last Stock Ledger Entry's `qty_after_transaction`. - Line 322 uses `"posting_date desc, creation desc, name desc"`, which adds `name` as a third key. - `erpnext/stock/doctype/stock_closing_entry/stock_closing_entry.py`, line 345: `"to_date desc, creation desc"`. - `erpnext/accounts/report/gross_profit/gross_profit.py`, around line 1017: query-builder `orderby(posting_date, desc)` followed by a second `orderby`. - `erpnext/accounts/doctype/bank_clearance/bank_clearance.py`, lines 301, 340 and 381: `posting_date` followed by `name`. - The same ordering also appears in tests: `test_stock_entry.py`, `test_stock_balance.py` and `test_stock_reconciliation.py`.

final answer
**The pattern.** A date alone isn't a total order. When several rows share it, `ORDER BY date DESC LIMIT 1` can return any of them. ERPNext adds tie-breaker columns after the date, in the same direction: - `date desc, creation desc`, and - `date desc, creation desc, name desc` where `creation` can also collide. `creation` is the row's insert timestamp. `name` is the primary key, so it is unique and makes the order total. **Why it matters across engines.** MariaDB/MySQL and Postgres break ties differently. With a non-total order, each engine can return a different row depending on its plan, index scan order or physical order. The two engines can then pick different rows, and so can two runs on the same engine. For something like `incoming_rate`, that changes the valuation. The code comments say as much: "total order so the latest SLE is picked identically on both engines" and "deterministic LIMIT-1 tie-break across engines". The tie-break makes the same row win on every engine. Postgres adds a related constraint. For `SELECT DISTINCT`, the ORDER BY columns must appear in the select list. `rfq_transaction_list` in `controllers/website_list_for_contact.py` selects `creation` for that reason. **Where it is applied:** - `erpnext/stock/stock_ledger.py`, `update_entries_after.get_incoming_value_for_serial_nos`. It uses frappe.qb with `.orderby(posting_date desc)`, then `.orderby(creation desc)`, then `.orderby(name desc)`, then `.limit(1)`. This is the clearest case. The query finds the latest Stock Ledger Entry for a serial number that was transferred to another company. - `erpnext/stock/stock_balance.py`, `set_stock_balance_as_per_serial_no`. It uses `order_by="posting_date desc, creation desc, name desc"`, `limit=1`. A line 82 query in the same file uses `posting_datetime desc, creation desc`. - `erpnext/stock/doctype/item_standard_cost/item_standard_cost.py`, `get_item_standard_rate`. It uses `effective_date desc, creation desc`, `limit=1`. Line 265 in the same file uses the same order. - `erpnext/stock/doctype/stock_closing_entry/stock_closing_entry.py`, around line 345. It uses `to_date desc, creation desc`. - A raw SQL query at `stock_ledger.py` line 1959. It uses `order by posting_datetime desc, creation desc`. - `erpnext/regional/report/irs_1099/irs_1099.py`, `get_payer_address_html` and `get_street_address_html`. These aren't date-based. They use the same idea: a trailing `.orderby(address.name)` or `.orderby(link.parent)` before `.limit(1)`, after the Case-based priority ordering. **Tests.** `test_stock_ledger_entry.py::test_incoming_value_for_transferred_serial_no_is_deterministic` creates two same-date SLEs, `MAT-SLE-TIE-A` (rate 100) and `MAT-SLE-TIE-B` (rate 200). It asserts that the one with the larger name wins, giving 200. Other tests use `posting_datetime desc, creation desc` too, for example in `test_stock_entry.py` and `test_stock_balance.py`. **A place without the tie-break.** `repost_accounting_ledger.py::validate_for_closed_fiscal_year` orders by `period_end_date desc` and `posting_date desc` with `limit=1`. It doesn't need a tie-break because it plucks only the date value, which is the same whichever tied row is returned.

final answer
**The pattern.** The code adds a second sort key to the `ORDER BY`. The key is almost always `creation`, the row's insert timestamp. The date sort stays first and the tie-breaker follows it, so the query reads `ORDER BY <date> DESC, creation DESC LIMIT 1`. One call site uses `name desc` as a third key. **Why it matters across engines.** A date is not unique. Several Stock Ledger Entries, Item Standard Costs or closing entries can share the same day or the same `posting_datetime`. SQL does not define the order of tied rows. MariaDB/MySQL, Postgres and SQLite can each return a different tied row, and the same engine can change its answer with the plan, an index or a version. With `LIMIT 1` that would give a different "latest" row depending on the database or the query plan. For stock queries this would give wrong running balances, valuation rates or `qty_after_transaction`. `creation` is a fixed, effectively monotonic value, so adding it gives the same row on every engine. I did not find a shared helper for this. Each call site writes the tie-breaker itself, either as an `order_by` string or inside raw SQL. **Where it is applied (non-test code):** - `erpnext/stock/stock_ledger.py`: the raw SQL that fetches the previous Stock Ledger Entry uses `order by posting_datetime desc, creation desc limit 1 for update`. It also uses `creation < ...` as a voucher condition. The function name was not visible in the lines I read. - `erpnext/stock/stock_balance.py`: `get_balance_qty_from_sle()` orders by `posting_datetime desc, creation desc` with `limit=1`. A second occurrence at line 322 adds `name desc` as a third key. - `erpnext/stock/doctype/item_standard_cost/item_standard_cost.py`: - `ItemStandardCost.get_last_standard_cost()` orders by `effective_date desc, creation desc`. - `get_item_standard_rate()` uses the same ordering for the latest submitted rate on or before the posting date. - `erpnext/stock/doctype/stock_closing_entry/stock_closing_entry.py`: `get_last_stock_closing_entry()` orders by `to_date desc, creation desc`. The same ordering appears in tests, which keeps the assertions stable: - `test_stock_entry.py` - `test_stock_reconciliation.py` - `test_stock_balance.py` The query-builder code uses the same chained idea. It appends `name` after the primary sort, for example in `bank_clearance.py`, `payment_entry.py` and `controllers/queries.py`. Those queries are not `LIMIT 1` picks, so this is a related use rather than part of the pattern above.

final answer
Within the code I inspected, there's no single shared helper or comment that guarantees this. It's a convention: you add `creation` as a secondary descending sort key after the date column. I did not open every call site, so the list below comes from searching for the pattern. **The pattern** Sorting only by a date, as in `ORDER BY posting_date DESC LIMIT 1`, leaves the order of rows with equal dates undefined. The queries append `creation DESC`, and in a few places `name DESC`, so the sort is fully ordered. `creation` is a timestamp with sub-second precision, which means "latest" resolves to the most recently created row. There are two forms: - **`frappe.get_all` / `frappe.db.get_value`:** a string such as `order_by="effective_date desc, creation desc"` together with `limit=1`. - **Query builder (`frappe.qb`):** a chained call such as `.orderby(date_col, order=Order.desc).orderby(sle.creation, order=Order.desc)`. Where an `.orderby(..., sle.creation, ...)` call is shown in the grep output, I haven't confirmed its date key. **Why it matters across database engines** ERPNext runs on MariaDB and PostgreSQL. SQL doesn't define the order of rows that tie on the sort key, so each engine and query plan can return a different one. The same data can therefore give different "latest" rows depending on the engine or index used. I'm inferring this reason from how SQL behaves; the code I searched has no comment that states it. **Where it is applied** - `erpnext/stock/doctype/item_standard_cost/item_standard_cost.py` - `ItemStandardCost.get_last_standard_cost` uses `order_by="effective_date desc, creation desc"` with `limit=1`. - `get_item_standard_rate` uses the same ordering. It picks the standard rate in force on a posting date. - `erpnext/stock/stock_balance.py` has two sites. One uses `posting_datetime desc, creation desc` (line 82). The other uses `posting_date desc, creation desc, name desc` (line 322), which adds `name` as a final tie-break. - `erpnext/stock/doctype/stock_closing_entry/stock_closing_entry.py` uses `to_date desc, creation desc` (line 345). - Tests use the same ordering: `erpnext/stock/doctype/stock_entry/test_stock_entry.py`, `erpnext/stock/report/stock_balance/test_stock_balance.py`, and `erpnext/stock/doctype/stock_reconciliation/test_stock_reconciliation.py`. - `creation` appears as a descending sort key in query-builder code in: - `erpnext/stock/stock_ledger.py` (lines 1654 and 2163) - `erpnext/stock/utils.py` (line 522) - `erpnext/stock/serial_batch_bundle.py` - `erpnext/stock/deprecated_serial_batch.py` - `erpnext/stock/doctype/bin/bin.py` - `erpnext/stock/doctype/stock_reconciliation/stock_reconciliation.py` - `erpnext/manufacturing/doctype/bom/bom.py` - `erpnext/accounts/report/gross_profit/gross_profit.py`, which orders by `warehouse, posting_datetime, creation` descending (line 1226)

final answer
On PostgreSQL, ERPNext serializes work per (item, warehouse) with advisory locks. It takes these locks before the row-level `FOR UPDATE` locks, so jobs queue up instead of deadlocking or computing from the same stale data. I read the code but didn't run it on Postgres. There are three layers, all gated on `frappe.db.db_type == "postgres"`. 1. **Repost gate: `repost_gate(item_code, warehouse)` in `erpnext/stock/stock_ledger.py`.** - It takes a session-level `frappe.db.advisory_lock(("stock_repost", item_code, warehouse), timeout=REPOST_LOCK_TIMEOUT)`. The timeout is 300s. - `repost_future_sle` wraps its per-pair repost in `with repost_gate(item_code, warehouse):` (around line 335). - Two background reposts of the same pair therefore take turns. Without the gate they would take the inner `for update` row locks in different orders and deadlock. - The key is a tuple, so a colon in an item code or warehouse name can't make two different pairs collide. - The docstring says row locks still enforce correctness. The gate only cuts deadlock and retry churn. - It deliberately doesn't gate the synchronous `repost_current_voucher` submit path. That path keeps relying on the existing deadlock retry. - The 300s timeout is well under the 1800s job timeout. A contended worker raises a recoverable `QueryTimeoutError`, re-queues, and frees its slot. - If `frappe.db` has no `advisory_lock`, `hasattr` returns `nullcontext()`, so there is no gate and no failure. 2. **SLE write gate: `sle_processing_gate(item_code, warehouse)` in the same file.** - It takes a transaction-scoped `frappe.db.transaction_advisory_lock(("stock-sle", item_code, warehouse), timeout=300)`. The lock is re-entrant and released at commit or rollback. - It is called at line 120 (per pair) and at line 613 (`self.args.warehouse`, in the SLE processing code). - MariaDB gets this serialization from gap locks on the previous-SLE locking reads. Postgres locking reads can't see rows another transaction is still inserting. - Without this gate, two writers would compute from the same stale previous SLE, and one Bin write would be lost. 3. **Batch valuation lock: `calculate_avg_rate` in `erpnext/stock/serial_batch_bundle.py`.** - This is the batch average-rate computation, in the `else` branch for outward entries (`actual_qty <= 0`). - Before `get_batch_stock_before_date()` reads the batch history, it calls `frappe.db.transaction_advisory_lock(("batch-valuation", sle.item_code, sle.warehouse))`. - Only one transaction at a time computes the batch average rate for that item-warehouse, so the rate isn't computed twice from the same snapshot. - The comment explains why this lock is used. Postgres has no gap locks, and row-locking the whole history would write a lock marker on every tuple. - In `erpnext/stock/deprecated_serial_batch.py`, the grouped queries skip `.for_update()` on Postgres, because the caller holds the advisory lock instead. MariaDB still uses the row lock. A test in `test_serial_and_batch_bundle.py`, `test_outward_batch_valuation_takes_transaction_advisory_lock`, checks that the batch lock is actually taken.

final answer
On PostgreSQL, ERPNext uses three layers of Postgres advisory locks, all keyed on the item-warehouse pair. Frappe's own row-level `FOR UPDATE` locks stay in place as the correctness backstop. I read the lock functions directly. I did not read every call site, such as the code around `stock_ledger.py:120` and `:613`. **1. Repost-vs-repost gate: `repost_gate(item_code, warehouse)` in `erpnext/stock/stock_ledger.py`** - It is called from `repost_future_sle`, around line 335, as `with repost_gate(...)`. - On Postgres it takes a session-level `frappe.db.advisory_lock(("stock_repost", item_code, warehouse), timeout=REPOST_LOCK_TIMEOUT)`. The timeout is 300 seconds. - Background reposts of the same pair therefore queue up. Without it they race into the inner `... for update` row locks in opposite order and deadlock. - The key is a tuple, so a colon in an item code or warehouse name can't make two different pairs share a lock. - The gate covers repost jobs only. The synchronous submit path (`repost_current_voucher`) is not gated, so a submit never blocks behind a background repost. It relies on the existing deadlock retry. - On MariaDB, or on a Frappe version without `advisory_lock`, the function returns `nullcontext()`, so there is no gate. **2. Per-write gate: `sle_processing_gate(item_code, warehouse)` in `stock_ledger.py`** - It is called at line 120 and at line 613 (inside `update_entries_after`, on `self.item_code` and `self.args.warehouse`). - It takes a transaction-scoped `frappe.db.transaction_advisory_lock(("stock-sle", item_code, warehouse), timeout=...)`. The lock is re-entrant and released at commit or rollback. - MariaDB gets this from gap locks taken by the previous-SLE locking reads, which block concurrent inserts. Postgres locking reads can't see rows another transaction is still inserting. - Without the gate, two writers would compute from the same stale previous Stock Ledger Entry, and the loser's Bin write would be lost. The gate prevents that double computation. **3. Batch average rate: `BatchNoValuation.calculate_avg_rate` in `erpnext/stock/serial_batch_bundle.py`** - For outward entries on Postgres it first takes `frappe.db.transaction_advisory_lock(("batch-valuation", sle.item_code, sle.warehouse))`. - Only after that does `get_batch_stock_before_date()` sum the history for the average rate. - MariaDB serializes through `.for_update()` on the grouped query, which relies on gap locks. Postgres has no gap locks, and `FOR UPDATE` is invalid with `GROUP BY`. Row-locking the whole history would also write a lock marker on every tuple. So `get_batch_stock_before_date` skips `.for_update()` on Postgres and the advisory lock does the serializing. **Deadlock handling in `repost_item_valuation.py`** - `RecoverableErrors = (JobTimeoutException, QueryDeadlockError, QueryTimeoutError)` are classified by exception type, not by matching the MariaDB message "Deadlock found". Postgres says "deadlock detected", which the old string match missed. - A recoverable error re-queues the Repost Item Valuation as "In Progress" instead of marking it Failed. A test in `test_repost_item_valuation.py` covers this. **Related, not part of reposting:** `PickList.set_item_locations` takes `("pick-allocate", item_code)` advisory locks, sorted by item code so overlapping documents can't deadlock each other.

final answer
On PostgreSQL, ERPNext uses Frappe advisory locks, keyed by item and warehouse, on top of the normal `for update` row locks. Everything below is in `erpnext/stock/stock_ledger.py` unless noted. I read the code but didn't run it. **1. `repost_gate(item_code, warehouse)` serializes background reposts** - `repost_future_sle()` wraps each `update_entries_after(...)` call in `with repost_gate(item_code, warehouse):`. That call is where the average rate is recomputed. - On Postgres, `repost_gate` returns `frappe.db.advisory_lock(("stock_repost", item_code, warehouse), timeout=REPOST_LOCK_TIMEOUT)`. This is a session-level advisory lock. - Two reposts for the same item-warehouse take turns. This stops them acquiring the inner `... for update` row locks in different orders, which is what causes the deadlock. - The key is a tuple, not a joined string, so two different pairs can't collide on one lock because of a colon in a name. - The row locks still enforce correctness. The gate only cuts deadlock and retry churn. - It covers repost against repost only. The synchronous `repost_current_voucher` submit path isn't gated, so a submit never blocks behind a background repost. That path relies on the existing deadlock retry. - On MariaDB, or on a Frappe without `advisory_lock`, it returns `nullcontext()`. MariaDB keeps the plain deadlock-retry path. **2. `sle_processing_gate(item_code, warehouse)` serializes all stock writes** - It calls `frappe.db.transaction_advisory_lock(("stock-sle", item_code, warehouse), timeout=...)`. This lock is held for the transaction, can be re-taken by the same transaction, and is released on commit or rollback. - MariaDB gets this protection from the gap locks taken by its previous-SLE locking reads. Postgres locking reads can't see rows another transaction is inserting. Without the gate, two writers would compute from the same stale previous Stock Ledger Entry (SLE), and one writer's Bin update would be lost. - It is called in two places: - The SLE-making function, around line 120. It iterates `sorted({(item_code, warehouse)})`, so two vouchers touching the same pairs can't take the locks in opposite order. - The `update_entries_after` initialisation, around line 613. **3. Timeouts and recovery** - `REPOST_LOCK_TIMEOUT = 300` seconds, well under the 1800s repost job timeout. A worker stuck waiting gives up and frees its slot for other items. - In `repost_item_valuation.py`, `RecoverableErrors = (JobTimeoutException, QueryDeadlockError, QueryTimeoutError)`. - The `repost(doc)` function rolls back on an exception. If the exception is one of those types, it sets the status back to "In Progress" so it is re-queued. Otherwise it sets "Failed". - The check is by exception type. The old traceback string-match only recognised MariaDB's "Deadlock found" and missed Postgres's "deadlock detected". **4. Avoiding double-computing** - `RepostItemValuation.deduplicate_similar_repost()` and `skip_reposts_covered_by_dependents()` drop redundant reposts. - The gates ensure only one transaction recomputes a given pair at a time.

final answer
On PostgreSQL, ERPNext serializes concurrent valuations with a transaction-scoped advisory lock. This is in `BatchNoValuation.calculate_avg_rate` in `erpnext/stock/serial_batch_bundle.py` (around lines 818–845). I read that code but did not look at the Frappe implementation of `transaction_advisory_lock`. The description of its lock lifetime below comes from the code comment. **The lock** - For an outward entry (`actual_qty <= 0`) the code computes the batch-wise average rate from the history. On Postgres it first calls `frappe.db.transaction_advisory_lock(("batch-valuation", sle.item_code, sle.warehouse))`. - Inward entries (`actual_qty > 0`) skip this. They just read `total_amount` from the Serial and Batch Bundle, so there is no shared history to protect. - The lock key is the tuple of a fixed `"batch-valuation"` string, the item and the warehouse. Two jobs valuing the same item-warehouse queue on the lock. Jobs for other item-warehouses don't block each other. - According to the code comment, the lock lasts until the transaction commits or rolls back, so one job finishes writing its stock value difference before the next one reads the history. That is what stops two jobs from computing the average rate from the same history at once. **Why an advisory lock rather than row locks** - Per the comments, MariaDB gets this serialization from a grouped `FOR UPDATE` read in `get_batch_stock_before_date`, which also takes gap locks. - Postgres has no gap locks. `FOR UPDATE` is also invalid together with `GROUP BY`. - The comment says that row-locking the whole history on Postgres would write a lock marker on every tuple. The advisory lock avoids that. - A single lock per item-warehouse, always taken before the history read, also gives a consistent lock order. That helps avoid deadlocks from many row-lock acquisitions in different orders. **The reposting layer** - `erpnext/stock/doctype/repost_item_valuation/repost_item_valuation.py` defines `RecoverableErrors = (JobTimeoutException, QueryDeadlockError, QueryTimeoutError)`. `repost()` references it, so if a deadlock or timeout still happens, the job is treated as retryable. I did not read how `repost()` retries, so I can't say what it does on a recoverable error. - `erpnext/stock/stock_balance.py` `repost()` commits or rolls back per item-warehouse pair, so locks are held for a short time.

When a Period Closing Voucher is submitted and its GL entry processing is split across multiple background workers, how does the system ensure that two workers never pick up and process the same date/report-type row at the same time?

final answer
Two workers never take the same date/report-type row because a row is claimed in the database before any job is queued for it. Row-level locking and a status flag do this, and the worker checks the flag again before it starts. The code is in `erpnext/accounts/doctype/process_period_closing_voucher/process_period_closing_voucher.py`. Submitting a Period Closing Voucher creates a "Process Period Closing Voucher" document. It holds one "Process Period Closing Voucher Detail" row per processing date and report type: Profit and Loss, Balance Sheet, and opening balances. Each row starts with `status = "Queued"`. **Claiming a row** - Both `initialize_parallel_threads(docname)` and `schedule_next_date(docname)` pick work with a query on the detail table. The query filters on `parent = docname` and `status = "Queued"`. - It is ordered by `parentfield, idx, processing_date` and ends with `.for_update(skip_locked=True)`. That is `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`. - The `FOR UPDATE` part locks the selected rows. If another transaction is claiming the same rows at that moment, `SKIP LOCKED` makes this query skip them instead of waiting. So two concurrent schedulers get different rows, or none. - `initialize_parallel_threads` takes up to 4 rows (`threads = 4`). `schedule_next_date` takes 1. **Marking and enqueueing** - While it still holds the lock, the scheduler sets each claimed row to `status = "Running"` with `frappe.db.set_value`. - It then calls `frappe.db.commit()`, which the code comments say keeps the transaction short and avoids REPEATABLE READ concurrency errors. The commit is skipped under `frappe.in_test`. - After that it enqueues `process_individual_date` on the `long` queue with `enqueue_after_commit=True`. - Once the commit has happened, the row is no longer `Queued`, so later queries cannot select it. **Worker-side check** - `process_individual_date(docname, row_name, date, report_type, parentfield)` starts by reading the row's status. - If the status is not `"Running"`, it returns immediately. A paused or cancelled row, or one that was reset, is therefore not processed by a stale job. **Related behaviour** - The final summary job is guarded separately. `summarize_and_post_ledger_entries` is enqueued with `job_name=f"summarize_{docname}"` and only if `is_job_running(job_name)` is false. - `pause_pcv_processing` and `resume_pcv_processing` reset rows stuck in "Running" back to "Queued" or to a paused state. This lets stuck rows be picked up again deliberately.

final answer
Two mechanisms keep workers from picking up the same date/report-type row: a row-level lock taken when the row is selected, and a status flag set on the row before the job is enqueued. I read this in `erpnext/accounts/doctype/process_period_closing_voucher/process_period_closing_voucher.py`. I did not read `process_individual_date`, so I haven't checked whether it re-checks the row's status when it starts. **How the rows are set up** - Submitting a Period Closing Voucher creates a "Process Period Closing Voucher" document. - Its `validate()` calls `populate_processing_tables`, which adds one row per date and `report_type` ("Profit and Loss" and "Balance Sheet"). Each row starts with status "Queued". - These rows are `Process Period Closing Voucher Detail` records, in `normal_balances` and, for the first closing voucher, `z_opening_balances`. - `on_submit` calls `start_pcv_processing`, which calls `initialize_parallel_threads`. **How a row is claimed** 1. **Row lock.** `initialize_parallel_threads` selects up to 4 Queued rows (one per thread). `schedule_next_date` selects 1. Both use `.for_update(skip_locked=True)`, which is `SELECT … FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`. A concurrent transaction that runs the same query skips rows already locked and takes different ones. So two schedulers never receive the same row. 2. **Status flip.** Inside that transaction, each selected row is set from "Queued" to "Running" with `frappe.db.set_value`. The selection only looks at "Queued" rows, so later queries won't pick it up again. 3. **Short commit.** The code commits right away (`frappe.db.commit()` unless running in tests). This releases the lock with the "Running" status already saved. A comment in the code says this keeps the transaction on the voucher and detail rows short and avoids REPEATABLE READ concurrency errors. 4. **Enqueue after commit.** The job is enqueued with `frappe.enqueue(..., enqueue_after_commit=True)`, passing `row_name`, `date`, `report_type` and `parentfield`. Each worker therefore gets exactly one specific row that is already marked "Running". **Chaining the next row.** I did not read `process_individual_date`. `schedule_next_date` is one of its callers, so presumably each worker calls it when it finishes and claims the next Queued row the same way. **Finishing up.** When no Queued rows remain, `schedule_next_date` checks whether every row is "Completed". If so, it enqueues `summarize_and_post_ledger_entries` once. A job name `summarize_<docname>` plus an `is_job_running` check stops it being enqueued twice. **Pause, resume and cancel.** `pause_pcv_processing` marks Queued and Running rows as "Paused", which also frees rows stuck in "Running". `resume_pcv_processing` puts Paused rows back to "Queued" and restarts them. `cancel_pcv_processing` marks Queued rows "Cancelled".

final answer
Two workers can't pick up the same date/report-type row because of a row-level lock combined with a status flag. This is in `erpnext/accounts/doctype/process_period_closing_voucher/process_period_closing_voucher.py`. I read the code but did not run it. **Where the rows come from.** Submitting a Period Closing Voucher creates a "Process Period Closing Voucher" document. Its child table, "Process Period Closing Voucher Detail", has one row per processing date and report type. Each row is created with `status = "Queued"`. The report types are Profit and Loss and Balance Sheet, and there are also opening-balance rows. **How a row is claimed.** Two functions claim rows, `initialize_parallel_threads(docname)` and `schedule_next_date(docname)`. `initialize_parallel_threads` takes up to 4 rows at the start. `schedule_next_date` takes one row each time a worker finishes. Both do the same three steps: 1. They select detail rows with `parent = docname AND status = "Queued"`. The query is ordered by `parentfield, idx, processing_date` and ends in `.for_update(skip_locked=True)`, which is `SELECT … FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`. A concurrent caller skips any row another transaction has already locked, so it never waits on that row or gets the same one. 2. While still holding the lock, they set the claimed row's `status` to `"Running"`. 3. They call `frappe.db.commit()` to release the lock, then `frappe.enqueue(process_individual_date, …)` on the `long` queue. The enqueue passes `row_name`, `date`, `report_type` and `parentfield`. Because the row is already `Running` and committed, any later query for `Queued` rows won't return it. The code comments say the commit also keeps the transaction short to avoid REPEATABLE READ concurrency errors. **Check inside the worker.** `process_individual_date(docname, row_name, date, report_type, parentfield)` begins by re-reading the row's status. If the status is not `"Running"`, it returns without doing anything. This covers a row that was paused or cancelled after it was enqueued. Pausing or cancelling only changes rows that are still Queued or Running. **After the work.** When the worker finishes summarising the GL entries, it saves `closing_balance` and sets the row to `"Completed"`. It commits and calls `schedule_next_date`, which claims the next Queued row, so the workers chain one after another. When no Queued rows remain and every row is Completed, `schedule_next_date` enqueues `summarize_and_post_ledger_entries`. It uses a `job_name` of `summarize_<docname>` together with an `is_job_running` check, so that final step also runs only once.

final answer
The system uses a row-level database lock plus a status flag on each row. There is no separate lock table. The code is in `erpnext/accounts/doctype/process_period_closing_voucher/process_period_closing_voucher.py`. **How rows are claimed** - Each date/report-type pair is a child row, a `Process Period Closing Voucher Detail`. Its `status` starts as `Queued`. When `ProcessPeriodClosingVoucher.validate` calls `populate_processing_tables`, it creates these rows for both normal balances and opening balances. - `initialize_parallel_threads` picks up to 4 rows. `schedule_next_date` picks 1. Both run the same query: - It selects rows where `parent = docname AND status = 'Queued'`. - It orders them by `parentfield, idx, processing_date`. - It ends with `.for_update(skip_locked=True)`, which is `SELECT … FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`. - A row that another transaction has already locked is skipped, not waited on. Two concurrent callers therefore get disjoint sets of rows. - The caller then sets each claimed row to `Running` with `frappe.db.set_value`. This happens before the job is enqueued, so any later query no longer sees the row as `Queued`. - In `schedule_next_date`, the claim is committed immediately (`frappe.db.commit()`, skipped in tests). The code comment says this keeps the transaction short and avoids REPEATABLE READ concurrency errors. Committing releases the row lock, but the row is already `Running` and committed, so other workers cannot select it. - The work is enqueued with `enqueue_after_commit=True`. A worker therefore never starts before the `Running` status is committed. **Guard in the worker** `process_individual_date` starts by re-reading the row's status. If it isn't `Running`, it returns without doing anything. This protects against stale or duplicate jobs, for example if a row was paused or reset. **Chaining and recovery** When a date finishes, the job calls `schedule_next_date` again. Each worker keeps pulling the next `Queued` row under the same lock. Once no `Queued` rows are left and every row is `Completed`, it enqueues a single `summarize_and_post_ledger_entries` job. A `summarize_{docname}` job-name check (`is_job_running`) stops that job being enqueued twice. The resume and pause code also re-queues rows stuck in `Running` back to `Queued`, so the same locking applies to them again.

final answer
I've finished tracing the code and am writing up the answer now. **Where it happens** `make_stock_entry` in `erpnext/manufacturing/doctype/job_card/mapper.py` builds the "Material Transfer for Manufacture" Stock Entry from the Job Card. In `set_missing_values`, it loops over `target.items` and calls `set_previous_operation_serial_batch(target, item)` for each row. That function is in `erpnext/stock/doctype/stock_entry/services/manufacturing.py`. **How serials or batches are chosen** 1. **Guards.** `set_previous_operation_serial_batch` returns without doing anything in these cases: - The Stock Entry has no `work_order`. - The row has no `s_warehouse`. - The row's quantity (`qty × conversion_factor`) is zero or less. - The row already has a `serial_and_batch_bundle`, `serial_no` or `batch_no`. 2. **Candidate pool.** `get_previous_operation_output_sn_batch(work_order, item_code, warehouse)` builds the pool of available serials and batches. It returns an empty pool unless both of these hold: - The item is the `finished_good` of some Work Order Operation on that Work Order. This is what marks it as a semi-finished good, so ordinary raw materials never qualify. - The item has serial or batch tracking. 3. **Produced minus consumed.** - `_get_operation_sn_batch` reads the Serial and Batch Entry rows from submitted Stock Entries of this Work Order for that item and warehouse. Cancelled entries are excluded. - "Produced" means Manufacture entries with `is_finished_item = 1`, matched on `t_warehouse`. - "Consumed" means later entries matched on `s_warehouse`. - Serials are included only if they were produced and not yet consumed. - Batch quantities are produced minus consumed, kept only when above zero. 4. **Trim to the row quantity.** `_cap_pool_to_qty(pool, qty)` does the trimming: - For serials, it takes the first `cint(qty)` in order (`pool.serial_nos[:qty]`). - For batches, it walks the batches in order and takes `min(batch_qty, remaining)` from each until the row quantity is covered. 5. **Bundle.** `SerialBatchCreation(...).make_serial_and_batch_bundle()` creates an Outward Serial and Batch Bundle. It is created with `do_not_submit=True`, so it stays a draft. The row's `serial_and_batch_bundle` is set to it and `use_serial_batch_fields` is set to 0. **If fewer units are available than the row quantity** There is no error. The code fills only what is available: - **Serials:** the slice returns fewer serials than the quantity. - **Batches:** the loop ends with `remaining` still above zero. - **Row quantity:** the row keeps its original `qty`. The bundle is created with `"qty": flt(qty)` but contains only the available serials or batches. - **Shortfall:** the docstring says it is "left blank for the user". The user has to select the missing serials or batches manually, or reduce the quantity. - **Nothing available:** if the pool is empty, the function returns early and creates no bundle. The row stays blank. I read the code but did not run it. So I haven't confirmed whether submission or validation of the partly filled bundle raises an error later.
